# Unfilter digest 05 — baby and toddler sleep

2026-11-16. Share: voice/reddit-ur01.jsonl (403 quotes), voice/reddit-ur05.jsonl (380), and voice/reddit-ur10.jsonl (338): **1,121 quote records read in full**. The three ledgers’ “What these posts add” sections and the current README.md/synthesis/findings.md informed this comparison.

These posts were selected because earlier rules excluded their subjects. Counts here count **quotes**, never people or prevalence. Multiple extracts and linked threads can come from the same author; agreement between extracts is not independent corroboration. Clinical explanations, claims about harm, safety assertions, advocacy and outcomes remain attributed to their speakers. Source wording, spelling, doses, explicit distress, violence, self-harm, sexual content and numerical assertions are retained.

The digest reproduces the entire share, grouped by the pattern each extract most directly supports, so no distinctive evidence depends on a single illustrative example. Each pattern gives the finding, its destination, its relation to the current conclusion and the proposed change. A quote may support several patterns; further-support lists link to its full words at its primary location. Source speaker/stage labels are retained as metadata, including unknown stages; they are not new identity or diagnostic inferences.

The most consequential changes for the rewrite are the non-universal schedule-first sequence, the difference between baby sleep and parental recovery, concrete medical/physical limits, direct care and housing needs, flexible bedsharing goals, and fathers’ own emotional accounts. The present findings already restore these broad subjects; the additions below sharpen mechanisms and correct remaining generalizations.

## Pattern map

1. [A useful result can be a tolerable night, a short break or an earlier evening, rather than zero wakes.](#pattern-1) — Extends; §§1, 6.1, 9.1 and 10.
2. [Illness, travel, new skills and changed circumstances repeatedly interrupt sleep that had been working.](#pattern-2) — Confirms and extends; §§2, 4.5, 10 and 17.2.
3. [Nap transitions and wake-window calculations create conflicting choices, and the recovered accounts do not follow one universal first step.](#pattern-3) — Confirms the fit problem; contradicts the universal schedule-first wording; §§4.4, 6.2, 16.3, 17.2 and 17.9.
4. [Feeding problems can make standard night-weaning, upright-to-crib and “increase daytime calories” instructions impossible.](#pattern-4) — Extends; §§3, 6.2, 10 and 17.2.
5. [Parents seek explanations beyond habits and report treatment benefit, failure, adverse experiences and unresolved tests.](#pattern-5) — Extends; §3 and §§4.1, 8, 10, 17.2.
6. [The parent’s hair, skin, breast, hands and exact posture can become the required sleep prop.](#pattern-6) — Extends; §§4.6, 5.2 and 9.1.
7. [Pain, recovery, pregnancy, disability and unusually high sleep need restrict the available sleep arrangements.](#pattern-7) — Extends; §§9.1, 9.6 and 10.
8. [Shared sleep can mean affection, a valued ritual, emotional repair or a desired arrangement with one specific problem.](#pattern-8) — Confirms the revised segmentation; contradicts the surviving blanket claim; §§5.5, 9.5 and 10.
9. [Bedsharing is often an improvised response to waking and can leave the child, parent or both still awake.](#pattern-9) — Confirms and extends; §§2, 5.5, 9.5 and 10.
10. [Crib transfer and independent onset are separate tasks, and success at bedtime need not carry through the night or into naps.](#pattern-10) — Extends and supplies a counterexample to a sufficient-onset interpretation; §§2, 4.4, 6.1, 6.2 and 10.
11. [An attempt can stop because of vomiting, stool, choking, breath holding, loss of voice or injury, not simply a lack of persistence.](#pattern-11) — Confirms and extends; §§4.5, 6.1, 10, 17.6 and 17.9.
12. [Dangerous dozing, impaired driving and unsafe work can be the immediate reason a parent changes course.](#pattern-12) — Confirms and extends; §§1, 6.4, 9.1, 10 and 17.2.
13. [The parent may remain awake through checking, phantom cries and fear even after the baby sleeps.](#pattern-13) — Extends; §§4.3, 6.4 and 9.1.
14. [Parents describe depression, hopelessness, detachment and regret, sometimes with good baby sleep and substantial support.](#pattern-14) — Confirms the revised limit; extends it beyond remaining insomnia; §§1, 6.4, 9.1 and 10.
15. [Sleep conflict can involve yelling, rough handling, hitting, restraint, self-harm and fear of doing harm.](#pattern-15) — Confirms and extends; §§5.1, 6.4 and 9.1.
16. [Bereavement, NICU separation and childhood experiences change what crying and bedtime mean to the adult.](#pattern-16) — Extends; §§5.1, 6.4 and 9.1; add a dedicated history-and-loss subsection.
17. [Night care depends on real caregiver availability, agreement, health and behaviour, not a nominal shift plan.](#pattern-17) — Confirms and extends; §§5.1, 5.2, 9.2 and 9.6.
18. [A preferred caregiver can prevent relief, while a different caregiver sometimes settles the child more easily.](#pattern-18) — Confirms and extends; §§5.2, 6.2 and 9.2.
19. [Housing loss, crowding, emergencies and siblings can remove the sleep environment or the possibility of a standard plan.](#pattern-19) — Extends; §§2, 5.1, 9.3, 9.6 and 10.
20. [Daycare and relatives can change naps, feeding, sleep safety and how much control parents actually have.](#pattern-20) — Confirms and extends; §§3, 5.3, 5.4 and 9.6.
21. [Swaddle and sleep-space changes can trade one problem for another, prompting repeated experiments and purchases.](#pattern-21) — Confirms and extends; §§4.6, 5.4 and 8.
22. [Temperature can be a changing overnight workload, and parents disagree with standard layering charts.](#pattern-22) — Extends; §§4.6, 9.6 and 10.
23. [Crib escape and toddler-bed transitions turn the whole room, door and toilet access into part of the sleep question.](#pattern-23) — Extends; §§4.6, 9.4, 9.6 and 17.2.
24. [Tracking raises separate questions about privacy, measurement, reassurance and emotional cost.](#pattern-24) — Extends; §§4.3, 6.1 and 11.
25. [Parents ask for practical branches and examples, while generic advice can contradict the baby or even its own rules.](#pattern-25) — Confirms and extends; §§4.1–4.4, 6.1–6.3, 10, 11 and 17.3.
26. [Parents seek help within their own crying limits and encounter blame, conflicting certainty and attachment claims.](#pattern-26) — Confirms and extends; §§3, 5.5, 6.3–6.4, 7 and 11.
27. [Relief can come from time, play, reframing, nursing, a different caregiver or treatment as well as formal training.](#pattern-27) — Confirms contrary evidence and extends its detail; §§2, 4.2–4.5, 5.5, 9.1, 16.5 and 17.7.
28. [Sleep and care capacity can shape an ongoing pregnancy decision, not just plans for future children.](#pattern-28) — Extends; §9.1; add a family-planning and reproductive-decisions subsection.
29. [Parents describe relentless waking, long settling and contact-only sleep even when a day seems otherwise normal.](#pattern-29) — Confirms; §§1, 2, 6.2 and 9.1.

## Patterns and complete evidence

<a id="pattern-1"></a>
### 1. A useful result can be a tolerable night, a short break or an earlier evening, rather than zero wakes.

**Belongs in:** §§1, 6.1, 9.1 and 10. **Relation:** Extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “What she wants back is small and personal:”

**What should change / remain:** Keep this conclusion, but define the target in the parent’s own terms: two or three brief wakes after fifteen-plus, a first crib stretch, safe sleep between feeds, or an earlier evening even when the toddler already sleeps through. “Sleeping through” is used inconsistently, sometimes with one or two feeds; do not silently convert it to no wakes.

**Full evidence at this location: 13 quotes.**

<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="pattern-2"></a>
### 2. Illness, travel, new skills and changed circumstances repeatedly interrupt sleep that had been working.

**Belongs in:** §§2, 4.5, 10 and 17.2. **Relation:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “Illness, feeding, travel, a bed move and treatment changes accompany the return of waking.”

**What should change / remain:** Keep the sequence and its uncertainty. Add nursing strikes, infection recovery, allergic reactions, simultaneous room/garment changes, and a tree branch striking the house. Separate the parent’s explanation from demonstrated cause, and keep repeated training distinct from a small clock adjustment.

**Full evidence at this location: 37 quotes.**

<a id="sbu01-0104"></a>
**[sbu01-0104]** — new_mother; regression_4_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0104 -->
> For the last 3 days, the second she is put down on her back she is trying to roll onto her tummy. She can roll about 90% of the way there but can’t get over her shoulder, which naturally makes her upset. I help her the rest of the way and then she’s happy until she gets tired. She then cries until I roll her over onto her back, and then the cycle repeats. I am losing my mind. She has been trying to roll since 3 months but up until lately she has been mostly chill about it. She gets lots of tummy time and practice rolling each wake period. The problem is this newfound love of rolling and the 4 month sleep regression is making it increasingly impossible to get her to nap.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0304"></a>

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0304 -->
> Then winter hit. He has brought home a fresh virus from daycare every single week for the past 4 weeks, and before that had a run of teething which saw us up with him in pain and distress for hours overnight for at least a week. When he’s sick he wakes frequently fussing and crying, and I suspect last night he was teething as well as being congested. My husband and I are running on the smell of an oily rag and starting to feel like there is no point to anything we do - the capped naps (no more than 90 min, the cot settling (he goes into cot awake at bedtime and is generally asleep in 5 or so min) , the bedtime routine, the set wake up times irrespective of how shit the night was - because every night we’re being tortured for hours as our LO fusses, cries and either struggles to breathe or holds and rubs their head and cheeks. (Yes we use pain relief judiciously but honestly doesn’t achieve much.)
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0312"></a>

Haven’t been this sleep deprived since the newborn phase. Curious if your kid followed the “regressions” people talk about or if they slipped after events or illness.

<a id="sbu05-0339"></a>
**[sbu05-0339]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0339 -->
> She’s suddenly fighting every single sleep opportunity, both naps and bedtime. She’ll be perfectly calm in our arms, but the moment we put her in her crib, she starts screaming and crying.  
> Previously, we could put her down awake and walk away. Now, I’m sometimes spending 15–20 minutes beside her crib patting her, rubbing her face, and trying to help her settle. Even then, she’ll sometimes wake after only 30 minutes.  
> She’s also started waking around 1 AM and needing assistance to fall back asleep, which is very unusual for her.  
> We’ve tried adjusting wake windows, offering additional comfort, and keeping her routine completely consistent. Nothing seems to be making a meaningful difference.  
> We recently traveled, and initially thought that was the cause, but we’ve traveled extensively with her before without these issues. We’re home now, and the problems have continued.

We've struggled to get this back ever since.

<a id="pattern-3"></a>
### 3. Nap transitions and wake-window calculations create conflicting choices, and the recovered accounts do not follow one universal first step.

**Belongs in:** §§4.4, 6.2, 16.3, 17.2 and 17.9. **Relation:** Confirms the fit problem; contradicts the universal schedule-first wording.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “Every reader who looked found the same order. First the wake windows or the nap caps.”

**What should change / remain:** Restrict that observation to the earlier selected accounts. In this share dangerous chest dozing directly triggers training, reflux treatment is tried first, and one household changes heat through the night. A schedule may help but is not an established first response. Keep both shorter- and longer-window accounts and the consultant whose 6pm minimum bedtime conflicts with the baby’s last nap.

**Full evidence at this location: 41 quotes.**

<a id="pattern-4"></a>
### 4. Feeding problems can make standard night-weaning, upright-to-crib and “increase daytime calories” instructions impossible.

**Belongs in:** §§3, 6.2, 10 and 17.2. **Relation:** Extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “health, feeding and access shape the sleep question.”

**What should change / remain:** Add the actual constraints: dysphagia prevents replacing milk with water; required upright time leaves a baby deeply asleep before transfer; bottle aversion, low intake and allergies restrict substitutions; the baby may refuse more daytime milk. Preserve hunger-versus-comfort uncertainty and parents who retain night feeds against advice. Independent onset and night weaning are separate questions.

**Full evidence at this location: 53 quotes.**

She had a tongue and lip tie fixed a few weeks ago, but she still doesn’t do well on the bottle unless she’s swaddled and in a dark room or drowsy/tired. I use dr browns because I heard it was one of the best at preventing air, but I think I need to look into the bottle thing more. She’s 3 months and drinks 18 oz a day. <a id="pattern-5"></a>
### 5. Parents seek explanations beyond habits and report treatment benefit, failure, adverse experiences and unresolved tests.

**Belongs in:** §3 and §§4.1, 8, 10, 17.2. **Relation:** Extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “medication and supplements that helped, failed or stopped helping”

**What should change / remain:** Retain all directions. Iron reportedly partly helps one child, worsens sleep through constipation in another, and precedes a “supercharged” toddler in another; normalized tests/tubes can leave wakings unchanged. Include the melatonin success and stated 1mg dose with age unknown, formula-switch relief, reflux-treatment relapse, prescription hesitation, diagnostic dismissal and denied referrals. None establishes a clinical effect or a dosing recommendation.

**Full evidence at this location: 96 quotes.**

<a id="pattern-6"></a>
### 6. The parent’s hair, skin, breast, hands and exact posture can become the required sleep prop.

**Belongs in:** §§4.6, 5.2 and 9.1. **Relation:** Extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “a caregiver may be physically unable to sustain rocking, holding or a walking-based solution.”

**What should change / remain:** Expand physical capacity beyond lifting: scratch/pinch/teeth stimulation keeps the parent awake after the baby sleeps; removing hair, skin or a hand restarts crying. Distinguish enjoyed contact from painful or repetitive contact, including a parent’s own sensory sensitivity and ADHD/AuDHD. The method has to be executable by this adult, not just acceptable for the child.

**Full evidence at this location: 36 quotes.**

<a id="sbu01-0003"></a>
**[sbu01-0003]** — new_mother; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0003 -->
> Then around 10 months, his sleep needs were harder to figure out and it started an hour or longer for him to fall asleep. I was getting so touched out from fidgeting and nursing that long.  So I started using my phone to keep me distracted because I didn't want him to feel that energy from me as he was falling asleep. Well now I'm feeling like that did him a disservice. He could probably feel me checked out,  sense that I wasn't fully with him,  that my attention was elsewhere.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0005"></a>
**[sbu01-0005]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0005 -->

> \- Cries when I unlatch him, then rolls all over me seeking contact  
> \- Pushes me away if I try to cuddle, while still crying for me  
> \- Rocking calms him but doesn’t put him to sleep  
> \- Gets overtired and more upset the longer it goes on

<a id="pattern-7"></a>
### 7. Pain, recovery, pregnancy, disability and unusually high sleep need restrict the available sleep arrangements.

**Belongs in:** §§9.1, 9.6 and 10. **Relation:** Extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “Physical health, treatment, housing and the other caregiver’s capacity shape what is possible”

**What should change / remain:** Include low cots that a parent cannot safely reach, sciatica, a torn disc, inability to rock or transfer, pregnancy that prevents using a floor bed, autoimmune/chronic-fatigue limits and a parent who needs 10 hours and does best with 12–14 despite the toddler sleeping through. Willingness to buy equipment does not remove these constraints.

**Full evidence at this location: 21 quotes.**

<a id="pattern-8"></a>
### 8. Shared sleep can mean affection, a valued ritual, emotional repair or a desired arrangement with one specific problem.

**Belongs in:** §§5.5, 9.5 and 10. **Relation:** Confirms the revised segmentation; contradicts the surviving blanket claim.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “Content cosleepers want nothing fixed”

**What should change / remain:** Replace that blanket sentence: some want nothing changed, while others want to retain bedsharing and fix early-morning restlessness, constant latch or inability to leave. Include pleasure, the father’s purchased star-projector ritual, and contact motivated by guilt about lost breastfeeding after drinking. Do not turn closeness into evidence of consent to every part of the arrangement.

**Full evidence at this location: 45 quotes.**

<a id="sbu01-0004"></a>
**[sbu01-0004]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0004 -->
> At 15 months, we switched to full-time cosleeping (sidecar crib) because I was exhausted from multiple middle-of-the-night wakeups and crying. It helped — he cries less now and usually just whines for me or nurses to settle.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0008"></a>
**[sbu01-0008]** — parent_unspecified; baby_5_8_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0008 -->

<!-- /voice-quote -->

> We live in a house with my parents-in-law. We have a huge room and a wide bed and we sleep all three together: my wife and I on the sides and our son in the middle making sure no region of the bed is unused.  Before afternoon nap and before bedtime we have a ritual: my son shows us that he wants milk, I ask him who should bring him milk, he says "daddy", then my wife asks him who will he drink the milk with and he says "mama". Then I go and warm up about 100ml of milk, my wife makes herself comfortable on the top left corner of the bed, our son gets into her arms, I bring the milk, he drinks it and then he crawls down onto the bed and fairly quickly falls asleep. This works for over a year now.

<a id="pattern-9"></a>
### 9. Bedsharing is often an improvised response to waking and can leave the child, parent or both still awake.

**Belongs in:** §§2, 5.5, 9.5 and 10. **Relation:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “Cosleeping is not a stable segment or a guaranteed source of rest.”

**What should change / remain:** Keep necessity, discomfort, fear, repeated waking and failed attempts alongside positive accounts. Add the explicit rebuttal that bedsharing followed months of waking and did not cause it. Some babies will not bedshare at all; some treat it as play; others need to be on the body even when already beside it. Do not infer the cause from the final sleeping location.

**Full evidence at this location: 106 quotes.**

> It started after a couple of times when I accidentally fell asleep in the nursery glider while holding him during a night wake up. <a id="pattern-10"></a>
### 10. Crib transfer and independent onset are separate tasks, and success at bedtime need not carry through the night or into naps.

**Belongs in:** §§2, 4.4, 6.1, 6.2 and 10. **Relation:** Extends and supplies a counterexample to a sufficient-onset interpretation.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “Age alone is not enough.”

**What should change / remain:** Add the parent whose baby falls asleep wide awake independently yet repeatedly wakes screaming, and distinguish sleeping by oneself from falling asleep independently. Keep first-stretch, daytime and caregiver-transfer goals separate. Upright post-feed time and failed transfers need their own practical questions; a plan cannot assume one learned skill solves all sleep.

**Full evidence at this location: 59 quotes.**

<a id="sbu01-0046"></a>
**[sbu01-0046]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0046 -->
> From 6m-16m bedtime was maybe 15-20 min from lights out. Transfer to the crib was a breeze, he was completely unfazed. He would even put himself back to sleep at night some if he woke. Starting at about 16m, and getting progressively worse until now, bedtime has become a 90 minute ordeal. Transfers almost never work and he wakes screaming. It takes him 30+ minutes to fall asleep in our arms.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0047"></a>
**[sbu01-0047]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0047 -->
> If he does wake in the middle of the night recently, we’ve started just putting him back in the crib after an hour or two out of desperation and to take a little breather and he will cry for 30-45 seconds then just lay down and go to bed, so we thought, okay this must be cool with him? Maybe he doesn’t want to be rocked to sleep?
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0054"></a>
**[sbu01-0054]** — parent_unspecified; baby_9_12_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0054 -->

<!-- /voice-quote -->

Nothing is working, she might be ok for 2 minutes tops and the she will scream until I pick her up. The docs said she is worried because between the ages 2-6 months is when the risk for SIDS is highest and I want to do right by my baby but I am so exhausted.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="pattern-11"></a>
### 11. An attempt can stop because of vomiting, stool, choking, breath holding, loss of voice or injury, not simply a lack of persistence.

**Belongs in:** §§4.5, 6.1, 10, 17.6 and 17.9. **Relation:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “These are concrete accounts of why a method ended or was ruled out, rather than evidence that persistence alone resolves it.”

**What should change / remain:** Add cut frenulums, bleeding after a fall over rails, repeated stool/rash during timed intervals, passing out, croup concerns, heat during choking cries and a face striking the crib during a reach for comfort. One parent reports vomiting stopped after ending ten-minute crying periods and sharing bedtime with the partner. Preserve the stop questions and observed sequences; do not convert them into a validated restart rule.

**Full evidence at this location: 45 quotes.**

<a id="pattern-12"></a>
### 12. Dangerous dozing, impaired driving and unsafe work can be the immediate reason a parent changes course.

**Belongs in:** §§1, 6.4, 9.1, 10 and 17.2. **Relation:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “The parent’s requested outcome includes being able to carry on caring and working, beyond getting free time back.”

**What should change / remain:** Add falling asleep on a bench, a baby face down beside a pregnancy pillow, failed awake-care shifts, a totaled car, a reported near patient injury and urgent paid coverage. Keep actual incidents distinct from feared ones. These accounts also disprove a universal schedule-adjustment-first sequence.

**Full evidence at this location: 31 quotes.**

<a id="sbu01-0040"></a>
**[sbu01-0040]** — parent_unspecified; baby_5_8_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0040 -->
> Just wanted to share the scariest experience of my life. I put my 6 mo old baby down to sleep in our bed (I usually put her down in her crib) but I thought hey let me run to the restroom quickly, I’m sure she’ll be fine if I put pillows all around. My worst nightmare came true when I heard the thump from the room and her cries. 😭 We have hard tile floors and she hit the back of her head..    
>  When I picked her up, she stopped crying and went completely limp and pale. She felt weightless so I panicked and dialed 911. 😭 Thank Goodness paramedics showed up to my house pretty quickly and she came back about! Her vitals and blood pressure looked good but I decided to take her in to have a CT scan done just in case. CT scan was normal as well and it was nothing but a scare. 🙌🏻     
>  Parents learn from my experience and don’t trust pillows. Babies can roll in a second. Babies are very resilient but it’s always scary to go through this. 😪
<!-- /voice-quote -->

Above all I need tips for how to stay alert during night wake ups so we don’t fall asleep accidentally in a dangerous position. <a id="pattern-13"></a>
### 13. The parent may remain awake through checking, phantom cries and fear even after the baby sleeps.

**Belongs in:** §§4.3, 6.4 and 9.1. **Relation:** Extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “The body stays on alert after the baby improves:”

**What should change / remain:** Specify behaviours: dreamfeeding partly to prove the baby is alive; hearing cries in fans, wind and blood sounds; anxiety after a first full night; remaining unable to nap even with help. Fear may follow injury, allergy, stillbirth or newly identified health concerns. This is not simply insufficient baby sleep.

**Full evidence at this location: 31 quotes.**

<a id="pattern-14"></a>
### 14. Parents describe depression, hopelessness, detachment and regret, sometimes with good baby sleep and substantial support.

**Belongs in:** §§1, 6.4, 9.1 and 10. **Relation:** Confirms the revised limit; extends it beyond remaining insomnia.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “The accounts do not establish that a baby-sleep plan treats any condition.”

**What should change / remain:** Keep separate: distress attributed to sleep loss; distress despite enough sleep and an easy baby; reluctance toward treatment; benefit attributed to medication; therapy experienced as too slow for an immediate sleep crisis. Include suicidal thoughts and regret in the parents’ own words. Better baby sleep cannot be used as evidence of psychological recovery.

**Full evidence at this location: 63 quotes.**

These feelings usually intensify if baby is crying, or won't go down easy. I have a ton of mom guilt around 5 p.m., when I'm literally watching the clock, waiting for my partner to finish so I can catch my breath.  I've heard the terms postpartum anxiety and postpartum depression, and while the stubborn, perfectionist side of me is denying these labels, part of me thinks they tick the boxes.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0059"></a>

<a id="pattern-15"></a>
### 15. Sleep conflict can involve yelling, rough handling, hitting, restraint, self-harm and fear of doing harm.

**Belongs in:** §§5.1, 6.4 and 9.1. **Relation:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “The earlier account of guilt and lost identity omitted reported harshness and harm.”

**What should change / remain:** Keep thoughts, urges, admitted actions, allegations about others and explicit denials distinct. Add fathers’ murderous thoughts and guilt, a parent kicking holes in a wall, self-hitting, rough placement, spanking to protect a sibling’s sleep and taking a break after recognizing danger. Include the full care sequence and the sought alternatives, rather than reducing these accounts to generic frustration.

**Full evidence at this location: 49 quotes.**

<a id="pattern-16"></a>
### 16. Bereavement, NICU separation and childhood experiences change what crying and bedtime mean to the adult.

**Belongs in:** §§5.1, 6.4 and 9.1; add a dedicated history-and-loss subsection. **Relation:** Extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “Mental health is central to some requests rather than a nearby angle.”

**What should change / remain:** Add surviving-child screams recalling a baby daughter’s death, delayed first holding after hypoxia/cooling therapy, full-term stillbirth, and fathers’ memories of being punished for insomnia. Supported crying and leaving a child alone are explicitly different for some parents. Bereavement can also remove the other caregiver altogether. These histories are not interchangeable with a simple crying preference.

**Full evidence at this location: 17 quotes.**

<a id="sbu01-0099"></a>
**[sbu01-0099]** — partner; unknown.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0099 -->
> Our son was in her room (we sleep separately due to sleep issues, no affection issues) and she moved him to my room without a word. I heard it, and heard our son getting fussy, so I went upstairs and told her she should at the very least tell me when she does this, and she only responded annoyed with ‘well he’s not crying yet is he’. I didn’t feel like arguing so I just closed the door and that’s when she kinda snapped. She either smashed her hand on objections or objects against objects, I don’t know, but I just sat there in the other room holding my son feeling so so tired, more like drained, and sad. She opened the door shortly after, said something about cutting herself and went downstairs to actually do so. She went back upstairs after.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0194"></a>
**[sbu01-0194]** — new_mother; baby_2_4_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0194 -->
> Husband said that he wanted to see me naked and was trying to lean towards sex. I told him multiple times that we can't as i have a worsening bacterial jnfection down there (TMI I know...) and our baby was wide awake and needed me to help her nap.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0195"></a>
**[sbu01-0195]** — new_mother; baby_2_4_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0195 -->
> Afterwards, I fed my baby and put her to sleep. But I feel so guilty for not tending to her sooner. I know my words are all over the place. I just don't know how to approach this, or what to do. Do I feel like he coerced me? Yes and no? Im just so confused anymore...what do I do now?
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0228"></a>
**[sbu01-0228]** — partner; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0228 -->
> Man, what you said about generational cycles just hit me like a ton of bricks. Growing up around age 7, I struggled with severe insomnia. My dad couldn't handle it and would spank me just so I'd stop coming into his room and bothering him while he slept. The pain and fear kept me away, and I was left alone in my bed dealing with pure panic until 3 AM.
> Reading your comment made me realize why my son's nighttime meltdowns trigger such a visceral, intense panic/stress response in me. The evening was coded as a time of rejection and distress for me as a kid.
> Reframing his cry as 'I'm uncomfortable and need my dad' instead of feeling triggered changes everything. Thank you so much for shedding light on this—it gives me a clear direction to dig deeper and actually heal this. Truly appreciate it, brother. Love you.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0235"></a>
**[sbu01-0235]** — expecting_mother; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0235 -->
> I slept in the room with my toddler because we fought last night and I didn’t want to sleep in the same bed as him. He was almost an hour late for work. He leaves the house and immediately starts belittling me and calling me names over text. He called me and I answered and he’s screaming at me that he’s going to get fired and so on and I hung up and blocked him.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

**[sbu01-0346]** — new_mother; baby_9_12_months.

I can handle some supported crying, but leaving him alone to scream feels very challenging.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="pattern-17"></a>
### 17. Night care depends on real caregiver availability, agreement, health and behaviour, not a nominal shift plan.

**Belongs in:** §§5.1, 5.2, 9.2 and 9.6. **Relation:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “These are different from two willing caregivers needing the same script.”

**What should change / remain:** Add shifts that collapse through work hours, both adults waking anyway, a father managing nights and work while ill, sexual coercion concerns, a handover followed by self-harm, and one parent reaching safety after domestic violence. Describe the two-mother household in its own terms; do not infer that the nursing parent’s partner must be a father. Fathers’ own emotional lives need space alongside process reports.

**Full evidence at this location: 54 quotes.**

<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="pattern-18"></a>
### 18. A preferred caregiver can prevent relief, while a different caregiver sometimes settles the child more easily.

**Belongs in:** §§5.2, 6.2 and 9.2. **Relation:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “the preferred caregiver pattern limits evenings, breaks and the ability to start or maintain a method.”

**What should change / remain:** Include the parent’s trouble trusting others, guilt when the baby settles for a nanny or father, and the distinction between doing a shift and having a workable soothing technique. Preserve contrary handovers: Dad’s supported settling, a nanny calming immediately, or a baby soothed by another family member’s voice.

**Full evidence at this location: 15 quotes.**

<a id="sbu01-0001"></a>
**[sbu01-0001]** — parent_unspecified; baby_5_8_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0001 -->
> I’m so sad. My hormones are going off making me so teary despite pumping, I feel rejected (silly I know) and frustrated. He won’t accept just cuddles or rocking/swaying etc to sleep from me like he does his dad, but he’s not here in the day time so he’s just crying and crying and crying and I’m heartbroken. Eventually he fell asleep in the car with me singing lullabies for his naps. I feel like I’m being cruel because I know he wants to cuddle up and feed and get snuggly but he obviously won’t latch so I’m stuck. It’s been days now and I fear this could be the end?
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0037"></a>
**[sbu01-0037]** — new_mother; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0037 -->
> I also do the night wakings because my baby doesn’t accept my husband at night. He wakes up with me in solidarity but honestly it’s just annoying half the time. I feel supported but exhausted, and unable to achieve any of my goals.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0113"></a>
**[sbu01-0113]** — partner; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0113 -->
> The dentist said his molars are about to erupt. I’m hoping this is just a temporary phase, but I’m wondering if I should keep trying to calm him myself, since right now none of us is sleeping.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0148"></a>
**[sbu01-0148]** — partner; newborn_0_8_weeks.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0148 -->
> Our baby has colic according to the Dr, she is very hard to keep calm or even make sleep a wink from 8:30pm - 11:30pm or 1am. This is leaving me feeling very defeated because it's always during my time that she is screaming and crying, I have tried everything I can think of to help her with this time, all kinds of holds, singing, different environmental changes, gas drops, etc and it just doesn't seem to matter.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0225"></a>
**[sbu01-0225]** — partner; toddler_24_36_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0225 -->
> Now, every single night (unless she's sleeping over at her grandparents'), she wakes up sobbing. It’s a 50/50 coin toss whether she just needs some quick reassurance and her pacifier, or if she needs to co-sleep with Dad to fall back asleep.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0073"></a>
**[sbu05-0073]** — new_mother; baby_9_12_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0073 -->
> My daughter has an extreme caregiver preference and SCREAMS when her dad tries to get her back to bed at night, or down for a nap and it can go on for almost 30 minutes before he can’t take it anymore and I go in there at which point she’s hyperventilating and it’s even harder for me to get her to sleep. i haven‘t consistently slept longer than 4 hours in 9 months, and haven’t even slept through the night since before I was pregnant. I am legitimately losing my mind. Seriously, what do I do????
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0088"></a>
**[sbu05-0088]** — parent_unspecified; regression_4_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0088 -->
> My partner does 1-2 feeds/changes when he’s home otherwise it’s me, baby sleeps on me during the day, I do majority of housework and cooking so I don’t really have much alone time.   
> We live with my mum and dad so I can have the support until my little one is older and they keep him entertained when I need to do housework, basic self care ect..  
> But oh god am I struggling, I don’t regret having a baby by any means but it is so difficult having a baby constantly in pain and crying.   
> I think I don’t accept much help because I’m way too fine tuned into his needs that I can’t trust others to care for him the way I do.   
> He doesn’t settle for anyone other than me, even my partner can’t settle him but I think that’s because I have that complete control and I can’t let go.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0110"></a>
**[sbu05-0110]** — parent_unspecified; newborn_0_8_weeks.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0110 -->
> 5 weeks reporting in. I was losing my mind with sleepless nights and absolute inconsolable crying. Then hubby told me what days are like: simple. I thought we were raising two different kids. His trick to get baby to settle: over-burping. I was like, I literally do not have the muscle mass for that. Had hubby show me his burping method. Repeated everything last night with my muscle accommodations. I just mentally think of this as "beat the shit out of kid". Like, 30 min of burping: patting, upright, over the shoulder hip hold, walking while over the shoulder hip hold, etc.
> 
> 
> 
> And baby loved it. ​I felt like I had my baby back and not some night monster. Its still laborious, but the extra minutes of "beating the shit out of kid" or burping helped him settle faster. Any whining, its right back up, PAT PAT PAT PAT. 
> 
> 
> 
> Keep patting for sleep (I guess)
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0314"></a>
**[sbu05-0314]** — new_mother; baby_9_12_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0314 -->
> After 10 months of hourly/bi-hourly wakes, needing to nurse every single time and not settling, my man woke up yesterday and decided he was DONE with me getting no sleep, little dude ending up in our bed around 2am every night, and the constant screaming if anyone but me tried to settle him. He said, “Tonight is the night we sleep train. You sleep, Daddy’s got it.”
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu10-0024"></a>
**[sbu10-0024]** — new_mother; baby_9_12_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu10-0024 -->

<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu10-0040"></a>

<!-- voice-quote:sbu10-0040 -->

It’s almost like he can’t fully wake up, similar to nighttime wake ups where he’s only awake enough to scream for a bottle and then immediately passes back out. He is very difficult to calm down during these episodes, but sometimes he can be snapped out of them by hearing my husband or my older son.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu10-0087"></a>
**[sbu10-0087]** — new_mother; unknown.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu10-0087 -->
> There were nights when my baby cried for 20 mins when I had her and the moment her nanny took her she stopped crying. I felt like a complete failure and I broke down that night.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

**[sbu10-0122]** — new_mother; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu10-0122 -->

While it did occasionally happen before the room share, it has gotten 10x worse. It's draining and dysregulating for everyone and doesn't feel like it's getting better.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="pattern-19"></a>
### 19. Housing loss, crowding, emergencies and siblings can remove the sleep environment or the possibility of a standard plan.

**Belongs in:** §§2, 5.1, 9.3, 9.6 and 10. **Relation:** Extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “Their posts open by ruling out the standard advice, which assumes space, a partner or control of the schedule”

**What should change / remain:** Add homelessness in a car while awaiting a tent and mattresses, flood displacement without a baby bed, frightening storms, a tree hitting the house, and grandparents’ homes without enough rooms. Distinguish structural absence, threat and bereavement from a routine lapse. Sibling protection can escalate adult actions and force split-room care.

**Full evidence at this location: 35 quotes.**

<a id="sbu01-0029"></a>
**[sbu01-0029]** — experienced_mother; unknown.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0029 -->
> My husband is away for work, my 3yo already cosleeps and now my 8yo has been coming in to my room after bedtime because of nightmares/growing pains/whatever. I understand she needs emotional regulation with her dad away but damn. 8-10pm is the only time of day when the house is quiet, when my space feels safe and I can focus on me, and now that gets eaten into as well.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0038"></a>
**[sbu01-0038]** — new_mother; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0038 -->
> My parents place also doesn’t have enough bedrooms for all of us, but we stay there because it would break their heart if we stayed at a hotel and financially we also just can’t afford that. I have to sneak into the room after my baby is in deep sleep so that I don’t wake him up. Sometimes I sleep on the couch. Which is fine if it weren’t for my brothers going in and out of the house all the time. The constant noise. I have asked a million time for them to be quiet but they just don’t get it. They don’t understand that I literally don’t get sleep.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0051"></a>
**[sbu01-0051]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0051 -->
> We have a 26 hour drive. Breaking up into 4 days. We spent today loading the trailer, and packing the rest of our stuff up. We’re currently sleeping on the floor, our dogs are being awful because they’re nervous that everything’s gone. 15m is sleeping in her pack and play, she’s still wide awake at 9pm. She was a terror today probably just unsettled that everything is gone. I’m going on like 4 hours of sleep. I still have to clean the house after my husband and our family are done loading. It took so much longer but we had no choice because my husband worked up until Friday. But omg my nerves are just shot, and now we’re gonna be in airbnbs for the rest of the trip idk how that’s going to go.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0053"></a>
**[sbu01-0053]** — parent_unspecified; unknown.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0053 -->
> I love my kids more than anything. I knew having two so close together was going to be hard, but I don’t think anything can actually prepare you for the level of exhaustion that comes with it. There is basically no time where someone doesn’t need me. Someone is hungry, someone needs a nappy change, someone is crying, someone needs settling, someone wants to be held, someone needs entertaining, and then somewhere in between all of that I’m supposed to eat, shower, sleep and somehow remain a functioning human being.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0157"></a>
**[sbu01-0157]** — new_mother; toddler_24_36_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0157 -->
> we co-sleep due to lack of space
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0159"></a>
**[sbu01-0159]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_24_36_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0159 -->
> He doesn't watch much TV unless his brother is napping (because he'll bang on the door or hit his brother in the face when I'm nursing). Each time his brother is sleeping I try to give all my attention to him thinking maybe it's a jealousy thing or he's acting out because he wants attention even if it's negative but after reevaluating my days I spend 90% of the time talking or playing with him and his brother is usually quietly playing across the room, on my hip or taking his nap.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0160"></a>
**[sbu01-0160]** — parent_unspecified; baby_5_8_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0160 -->
> It is SUPER stressfull for me and my baby, and we usually spend 2-3 days to recover from such an outing. Last time we were out I just took her away from the party and sobbed with her.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0262"></a>
**[sbu01-0262]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0262 -->
> However for the past two weeks, she’s still awake when he comes to bed. I watch her on the monitor jumping around, dancing, running, singing, throwing things out of her crib. Just having an absolute rager every night. Then when the time comes for her big brother to come to bed, she’s still wound up and gets him excited. They jump around together while he catches the toys she throws out of her bed, laughing and giggling. It was cute at first, but I let it go the first night to see how long they’d do it for and there seems to be no end. I eventually set up the pack & play in my walk-in closet and moved my youngest in there. This has been happening every night ever since.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0334"></a>

<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0343"></a>
**[sbu01-0343]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0343 -->

<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0356"></a>
**[sbu01-0356]** — parent_unspecified; baby_5_8_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0356 -->
> We are getting a suite with separate bedroom so baby could have her own space and we are willing to buy blackout curtains etc.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0038"></a>
**[sbu05-0038]** — experienced_mother; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0038 -->

<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0121"></a>

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0121 -->

<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0122"></a>
**[sbu05-0122]** — parent_unspecified; baby_5_8_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0122 -->

He ended up awake pretty late some nights bc without blackout curtains it doesnt get dark until almost 9pm by me.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0123"></a>
**[sbu05-0123]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0123 -->

Initially I thought night terrors but it’s looking like separation anxiety because every time I pick her up (she scream cries and kicks her legs) she automatically calm and sleeping. I have to wait for her to fall back into a deep sleep to transfer into the cot. 
> 
> Her cot is next to our bed and newborn is in his bassinet with us too. We’ve realised we need to split (me with 13m old and dad with newborn in another room). What I’m wondering is when does the separation anxiety stop? I know I need to co sleep if I actually want some sleep
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0132"></a>
**[sbu05-0132]** — new_mother; baby_5_8_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0132 -->
> I'm so backed up with pumping every 3-4 hours, breastfeeding him to help him nap or when I don't feel like dirtying a bottle, standardly caring for my son, caring for myself, and I live in a one bedroom apartment with two cats and two goldens.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0147"></a>
**[sbu05-0147]** — partner; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0147 -->
> You see, our babies are poor sleepers, especially the older one. We haven't slept a full night in almost two years. It's hard to put her to sleep, and she wakes up often, very dramatic about it. So much crying, and she wants to play at 1 AM or 3 AM or whatever it is. She also has separation anxiety. My wife sleeps with the newborn in another bedroom, while I share the king size bed (with safety railings and nets) with the toddler, who would never fall asleep without me by her side.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0153"></a>
**[sbu05-0153]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_24_36_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0153 -->

<a id="sbu05-0156"></a>
**[sbu05-0156]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_24_36_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0156 -->
> My eldest two are 3 & 4. They are currently sharing a bedroom out of comfort not necessity. When we tried separate rooms they were both too scared to sleep alone. I put them down for a nap around 1 PM with the door shut, not using any child lock devices at their request.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0189"></a>
**[sbu05-0189]** — experienced_mother; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0189 -->

His sleeping and living arrangement isn’t the way I want it, but that’s how it has to be for the next about 7 months.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0217"></a>

<a id="sbu05-0243"></a>

We have an older daughter and struggling to be there mentally for her (I.e. will to play as much as she wants) and really just need some advice.

<a id="sbu05-0367"></a>
**[sbu05-0367]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_24_36_months.

<a id="sbu05-0375"></a>
**[sbu05-0375]** — experienced_mother; baby_2_4_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0375 -->
> I’m so anxious and I’m so so exhausted. Last night husband slept with her in her room and I had baby. My body was so stressed I could feel my heart beating all night as I try to sleep. I know we just have to work through this I hope she eventually catches on. We need to move baby across from her room in a few months we can’t have her screaming or wandering. 
> 
> We’re going to attempt some sort of fade out method? Last night he laid with her till she fell asleep and ultimately slept with her bc she wouldn’t go back to sleep after waking. Tonight he’s sitting in the bed, tomorrow hopefully sitting next to the bed and so on?
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu10-0012"></a>
**[sbu10-0012]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu10-0012 -->
> My nearly 5 yr old wants his 12m old brother to share his room and ideally his bed. My baby really wants to cosleep, he wakes up a ton at night and I think he just needs someone. 
> 
> Can anyone tell me how they have navigated safe sleep with co-sleeping siblings?
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu10-0179"></a>
**[sbu10-0179]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu10-0179 -->

<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu10-0238"></a>
**[sbu10-0238]** — parent_unspecified; baby_9_12_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu10-0238 -->

<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu10-0244"></a>

<!-- voice-quote:sbu10-0244 -->

<!-- /voice-quote -->

**[sbu10-0248]** — new_mother; baby_5_8_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu10-0248 -->
> The problem is that his nursery is in the second story of our house (which we hoped to be his room when he’s a little older) but we’re worried about him being up there alone & also having to go upstairs if there are night feedings needed.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu10-0315"></a>

<!-- voice-quote:sbu10-0315 -->

Our one year old has been waking up every few hours, and our 4 year old is having a lot of anxiety and keeps asking for her bed. It’s so hard because no matter what we say to reassure her that she’ll have her bed back soon, I can see how worried she still is.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="pattern-20"></a>
### 20. Daycare and relatives can change naps, feeding, sleep safety and how much control parents actually have.

**Belongs in:** §§3, 5.3, 5.4 and 9.6. **Relation:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “Daycare: the place where naps "just work", or fall apart”

**What should change / remain:** Keep both sides but add reported injuries, fear of abusive responses to non-napping, conflict over blankets and stomach positioning, daycare refusal to offer a morning nap, and anticipatory work on preschool nap time/light. Reports and allegations remain attributed; daycare is not automatically the easy-nap setting.

**Full evidence at this location: 23 quotes.**

<a id="sbu01-0049"></a>
**[sbu01-0049]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0049 -->
> She left the baby inside the house to nap all alone in a room on the opposite side of where we were so there’s no way she could hear or see her while she napped. She didn’t have a monitor or anything.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0055"></a>
**[sbu01-0055]** — parent_unspecified; baby_9_12_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0055 -->
> I am afraid of trying to put her in a day care the way she is. I’m genuinely afraid they might get mean or abusive with her because she won’t nap. I talked to the pediatrician and she said she seems to be fine.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0072"></a>
**[sbu01-0072]** — new_mother; baby_5_8_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0072 -->
> My poor daughter, I take her to the peds ER (awful experience where they ridiculed me for saying that she was sick when she looked fine) and it turns out she had a double ear and eye infection. I don’t even trust the diagnosis because it sounded so ridiculous. But diligently I worked with the daycare, despite all the set backs to try and get her adjusted. I crib trained her, taught her how to take the bottle how THEY wanted instead of what she was used to at home.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0073"></a>
**[sbu01-0073]** — new_mother; baby_5_8_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0073 -->
> Last Monday they sent her home with a fever and it took her until this Monday to recover. I sent a happy, fed baby to daycare and when I pick her up they tell me “This has to stop. It cannot continue. She cries ALL day and we need to do something because she cries and cries. We think you don’t feed her enough” and when I take her into my arms her right eye is scratched and red, and there is a red bump all over the back of her head. When I asked how it happened the gave me the run around saying she was banging her head against the crib.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0079"></a>
**[sbu01-0079]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0079 -->
> The first two incidents took place at the beginning of April. One incident involved the educator in question pulling a child’s hair, the other incident included forcefully handling a child when they wouldn’t cooperate during nap time.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0090"></a>
**[sbu01-0090]** — experienced_mother; looking_back.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0090 -->
> Noone, especially new parents in the newborn trenches needs to listen to an older mother telling them to appreciate the time now. Who does that help? I didn't appreciate it back then, because it was hard. I'd be a hypocrite to expect differently from anyone going through it now.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0140"></a>
**[sbu01-0140]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0140 -->
> There were few weeks there where she seemed to do pretty well on the one nap, getting anywhere between 1.5 to even sometimes 2 hours in during that one nap. But recently things have gone off the rails. She rarely gets over an hour and 15 minutes of nap which means she’s awake by like 1:15 or 1:30pz And then when bedtime rolls around she is so overtired and refuses to be put down in her crib, but will not fall asleep in our arms either. She was previously fully capable of putting herself to sleep every night. There has been one or two nights a week lately where we’re spending 2 hours trying to get her to sleep.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0141"></a>
**[sbu01-0141]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0141 -->
> I’m at a loss for what to do. This has been going on for 4 weeks now and there seems to be no end in sight. We fix the over tiredness on weekends and then it’s immediately back the next day at daycare. We’re constantly on edge every evening.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0201 -->

> \- let him cry it out 
> 
> \-the dummy is going to make it worse

<a id="pattern-21"></a>
### 21. Swaddle and sleep-space changes can trade one problem for another, prompting repeated experiments and purchases.

**Belongs in:** §§4.6, 5.4 and 8. **Relation:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “temporary wins, failures, temperature and daycare constraints, and repeated reluctance to buy another short-lived fix.”

**What should change / remain:** Add failed size/fit options, rolling that forces a garment change, hip-harness restrictions, a bassinet chosen for mesh despite worse sleep, an uncertain mini-crib mattress, unreliable always-on sound machines and rails bought for shared sleep. An item being used or described as safe by a parent is not an established safety finding.

**Full evidence at this location: 44 quotes.**

<a id="pattern-22"></a>
### 22. Temperature can be a changing overnight workload, and parents disagree with standard layering charts.

**Belongs in:** §§4.6, 9.6 and 10. **Relation:** Extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “temperature and daycare constraints”

**What should change / remain:** Add one family’s sequence of cooler sleep onset, heat added after 30–60 minutes, several degrees of warming and a blanket, with inconclusive consultations/tests. Others report cold-room waking, heater clicks or improved sleep with less insulation. Do not collapse these opposite observations into one temperature rule or count linked sleepwear-scepticism threads as independent voices.

**Full evidence at this location: 12 quotes.**

<a id="sbu01-0069"></a>
**[sbu01-0069]** — parent_unspecified; baby_9_12_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0069 -->
> Maybe she is cold? The time she wakes is generally the lowest temp, but I’m not sure how to turn on a heater without waking her up anyways. and she has done this during the warmer days too (I am socal so we have had a really hot winter)
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0151"></a>
**[sbu01-0151]** — parent_unspecified; baby_2_4_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0151 -->
> My 3 month old likes to be warm at night and wakes often at night if cold and clearly the lullaby trust advice on what to wear doesn't apply to him 🙄 He is in a sleepsuit, short sleeved bodysuit, a 1 tog love to dream swaddle (with one arm out currently) and a cellular blanket and isnt sweaty and therefore not overheating.  Room is between 20 and 23 at night at the moment with the fan from our aircon unit on. When it gets hotter we have the air con unit on and set to 23 but the air is of course rather chilly the first half the night and once the room comes down in temp, it isn't on as much the rest of the night
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0152"></a>

I do also wonder if he is now getting used to the feeling the blanket tight against his belly (as it is tucked in) so could just be a sleep association that needs removing
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu01-0378"></a>
**[sbu01-0378]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0378 -->
> Oh, I definitely have to be in his bed with him to keep him there until 6:30. Unfortunately. He’s in warm pajamas and socks. We tried a space heater but they all click and wake him up when they start in the mornings.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0117"></a>
**[sbu05-0117]** — expecting_mother; unknown.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0117 -->
> First: for swaddles, I know that some countries are already recommending against them for safety reasons. Personally, I find the theory compelling enough (compression of chest causes deeper sleep, which is a risk factor for SIDS; moro reflex helps wake baby & therefore keeps them out of dangerous deep sleep), that I would rather go without from day one. Yes, I know baby may wake up more often, but again this seems protective so I'm fine with that. I will have both myself and my husband home for 12 weeks, so I figure worst case scenario we can sleep in shifts.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0118"></a>
**[sbu05-0118]** — expecting_mother; unknown.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0118 -->
> We typically keep our house at 74 degrees F (\~23 C), and I know recommended for SIDS protection is 68-72F. So why would we need an extra layer on top of a long sleeve/footie onesie? Couldn't we just keep our heat at 74 and skip the extra layer? Of course if baby is showing signs that she's cold I'd look into options like a sleep sack, but it seems like it's such a default for parents to use I'm wondering what on earth I'm missing here? Not to mention...they're quite expensive!
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0159"></a>
**[sbu05-0159]** — expecting_mother; unknown.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0159 -->
> But I cannot wrap my head around why, in the ideal temperature range, it would be recommended to put your baby in a onesie, footie pajamas, AND a 1 tog sleep sack? Why is the "ideal temperature" one that's cold enough you need multiple layers, vs a hotter temperature with fewer layers? The cynical part of me says the former sells expensive sleep sacks, which is why it's always these manufacturers that have the charts, and not the AAP (or similar governing bodies).
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0160"></a>
**[sbu05-0160]** — expecting_mother; unknown.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0160 -->
> Yeah, you're right. I'm trying to "science!" my way out of a question where the ultimate arbiter is going to depend on what baby needs when she gets here.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0346"></a>
**[sbu05-0346]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0346 -->
> We gradually turn on heating 30-60 minutes after she falls asleep, otherwise she gets cold and wakes up crying. You would ask how we know she is cold - she feels cold at back, chest, hands. During the night the temperature has to rise 2-3-4 degrees C and we usually put a light blanket on top of her, otherwise she wakes up all the time. So it is getting warmer where we live now, but up until recently in winter, she would fall asleep at 20-21 degrees C and the temperature would have to get to 23-24 C or she would wake up all the time. We have noticed that 2,5-3 hours after falling asleep she would get cold in like 15 minutes. I check her back -warm - 15 minutes later feels very cold.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu05-0347"></a>
**[sbu05-0347]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu05-0347 -->
> We took her to several doctors to ask. Our family doctor said that he did not know what to suggest and referred us to others. He did mention that it is normal for body temperature to drop in deep sleep (I read about it too) but not to wake up because of it. Other two doctors were terrible... Suggested we let her just cry... keep the room temperature stable all night(does not work)... ditch the sleeping bag and let her kick her blanket (she would be even COLDER)... because apparently we should not be scared to let her get cold as she would not get sick from it (never have I said getting sick was the problem)... It was very frustrating. We did some lab tests (hormones and ferritin) - 2 out of three doctors said were not concerning, the third didn't want to look... To me(not a doctor) and from what I read online, ferritin could be a little low. Doctors say not anemic and that's it.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu10-0067"></a>
**[sbu10-0067]** — parent_unspecified; unknown.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu10-0067 -->
> With the change in seasons, we switched from a 1.0 to 0.5 sleep sack for baby. Instantly her sleep has improved. I’m terrified I’ve been boiling our baby overnight for months. Long sleeve body suit + 1.0 sleep sack in 24C is how we’d kept her all this time. I feel such guilt. I really hope I haven’t caused cumulative brain damage or smth.
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="sbu10-0287"></a>
**[sbu10-0287]** — parent_unspecified; baby_5_8_months.

<!-- voice-quote:sbu10-0287 -->
> But I dress her pretty warm at night and bumped up the thermostat. She slept much better in the snoo when she was warmer too
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<a id="pattern-23"></a>
### 23. Crib escape and toddler-bed transitions turn the whole room, door and toilet access into part of the sleep question.

**Belongs in:** §§4.6, 9.4, 9.6 and 17.2. **Relation:** Extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “They describe bedtime battles, climbing out of the cot, a parent stuck in the room”

**What should change / remain:** Add rail gaps, trapped limbs, climbing despite a backwards sleep sack, a room that must be cleared for unsupervised access, door locks/restraints and head-banging, plus potty access and okay-to-wake clocks. Keep the 24–36-month tasks visible; not every toddler transition is another infant training attempt.

**Full evidence at this location: 30 quotes.**

<a id="pattern-24"></a>
### 24. Tracking raises separate questions about privacy, measurement, reassurance and emotional cost.

**Belongs in:** §§4.3, 6.1 and 11. **Relation:** Extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “perceived emotional cost and uncertain advice.”

**What should change / remain:** Add an outright request for an app that does not sell baby data; motion alerts that do not identify wake duration; settling time counted as sleep; a monitor missing vomit/stool in a dark room; oxygen alerts possibly caused by fit. Keep actual reassurance, uncertainty and obsessive checking alongside one another.

**Full evidence at this location: 13 quotes.**

<a id="pattern-25"></a>
### 25. Parents ask for practical branches and examples, while generic advice can contradict the baby or even its own rules.

**Belongs in:** §§4.1–4.4, 6.1–6.3, 10, 11 and 17.3. **Relation:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “Show that the answers changed the plan.”

**What should change / remain:** Add timestamped visible examples, when to pick up or stop tonight, how to transfer, which feed to retain and what to change first. Show the internal contradiction of a consultant’s earliest bedtime, not just generic-plan dissatisfaction. The TCB price deters a parent whose baby already has the skill the programme assumes is missing. An urgent request for a person or clinical reevaluation is not a request for a PDF.

**Full evidence at this location: 38 quotes.**

<!-- voice-quote:sbu10-0116 -->
> Is it a totally crazy idea to spend the first couple of months pretending we don't have circadian rhythms and truly "sleeping when the baby sleeps"? i.e. adopting a polyphasic sleep schedule instead of the usual monophasic schedule? <a id="pattern-26"></a>
### 26. Parents seek help within their own crying limits and encounter blame, conflicting certainty and attachment claims.

**Belongs in:** §§3, 5.5, 6.3–6.4, 7 and 11. **Relation:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion, exact excerpt:** “Parents are also trying to escape conflicting certainty and find practical help without being condemned.”

**What should change / remain:** Keep reported claims intact without adopting them: attachment parenting is not defined by cosleeping or breastfeeding for one speaker; others fear separation will damage attachment. Some reject both crying methods and bedsharing. Record safety advocacy and the source’s extraordinary “99%” wording as a speaker’s assertion, not a mortality fact. Keep self-blame and other people’s blame distinct.

**Full evidence at this location: 31 quotes.**

<a id="sbu01-0044"></a>

<!-- voice-quote:sbu01-0044 -->
> Actually baby’s pediatrician says they dont need to feed overnight, a lot of babies do it solely for comfort. A lot of babies over 1 month sleep through the night. As long as they get enough ounces during the day and arent dehydrated overnight it isnt an issue. Nothing personal but unless youre a pediatrician this isnt the place for medical advice
<!-- /voice-quote -->

<!-- /voice-quote -->
